# AI Papers of the Week — 2026

[← Back to main index](../README.md)

This page collects every weekly issue of **AI Papers of the Week** from 2026. For other years, see the [main index](../README.md).

---

## Top AI Papers of the Week (October 26 - November 1) - 2026
| **Paper**  | **Links** |
| ------------- | ------------- |
| 1) **DecisionRAG** - Whatever a retriever misses never reaches the generator, and most retrievers still rely on keyword matching or fixed embeddings. Researchers from Cornell and ASAPP propose DecisionRAG, which replaces the first-stage retriever with a decision model that walks the corpus from broad topics down to documents and passages. <br>● Hierarchical navigation: At each level, a decision model such as Jev scores every candidate in one forward pass without generating tokens, then keeps the most promising branches. Under a balanced hierarchy, the number of sequential decisions grows logarithmically with corpus size.  <br>● Shared context: Judging candidates together lets the model compare them against one another. This adds 7.7 R@1 points on MultiDoc2Dial and 6.5 Hit@1 points on Natural Questions over judging them separately.  <br>● Results: With no task-specific training, DecisionRAG beats dense retrieval plus an 8B reranker by 13.8 R@1 points on MultiDoc2Dial and 17.4 Hit@1 points on Natural Questions, which searches 2.68 million Wikipedia paragraphs. Retrieval takes 0.7 to 1.8 seconds and 0.2 to 2.3 cents per query.  <br>● Why it matters: A Qwen3-8B judge trained on in-domain data reaches 60.7 R@1 on MultiDoc2Dial, 5.7 points above the best compared published task-trained retriever. The method depends on a well-organized corpus hierarchy, and a branch pruned early stays lost unless budgets grow or dense retrieval adds candidates. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/retriever-free-retrieval-navigating-documents-with-decision-models-2610.35868), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2115810394614960516) |
| 2) **Unified Training** - Foundation models usually train in separate stages (pretraining, midtraining, supervised fine-tuning, and RL), each with its own recipe. Researchers from Carnegie Mellon, Johns Hopkins, and Aurora Innovation show that all of these stages can run under a single likelihood-based algorithm, which they call unified training. <br>● One shared loss: Suitable RL objectives are derived as maximum-likelihood fitting to model-generated targets, so RL generations and static data share one loss and one trainer. The design problem becomes which data to train on, in what proportions, and when, with staged training as one special case.  <br>● Reasoning puzzles: A 124M model trained from scratch beats staged training on the RL tasks and on larger puzzles it never saw in midtraining or RL. Under a fixed compute budget, unified training has the highest accuracy at the smallest and largest budgets, while staged training leads at intermediate budgets.  <br>● Math RL: On a 0.76B model, mixing in 0% to 5% midtraining data keeps math accuracy similar while retention improves. Coding accuracy drops as the mix grows.  <br>● Why it matters: After math training, all unified models beat staged training on final coding accuracy despite starting lower, and the 5% mix does best on all four metrics. The experiments are small-scale, and the authors have not found a mixture or schedule that works best across the board. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/training-foundation-models-with-a-single-algorithm-2610.40564), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2116629539623473617) |
| 3) **HarnessMM** - Agent harnesses often call the model again after tool feedback even when an earlier call already settled the next action. Researchers from Tsinghua University and Microsoft Research Asia introduce HarnessMM, which reuses those settled decisions and tunes harness configurations through recursive self-improvement while model weights stay fixed. <br>● Executable Decision Graphs: These graphs store exact actions, literal arguments, causal dependencies, and public guards, so a resolved decision can run again at a later tool boundary.  <br>● Guarded reuse: Guarded Decision Reuse activates a stored decision when observed tool receipts satisfy its guards, and Certified Callback Substitution executes it through the native interface and records that the slow call was removed. New decisions, unsupported evidence, and abstentions go back to the original model.  <br>● Results: On the same 98 HumanEval test tasks, DeepSeek Harness rises from 71.43% to 85.71% success while tokens per task fall from 96.28k to 11.62k, an 87.93% saving. Codex holds 90.82% success with 29.54% fewer tokens, and a separate MBPP ablation measures 19.52% lower cost than retained callbacks.  <br>● Why it matters: DeepSeek Harness drops from 954 to 415 paid model calls on HumanEval, which gives teams a reason to count repeated decisions before paying for a bigger model. Savings depend on provider cache accounting and on how many actions are already resolved, and authoring, selection, and recovery can outweigh the avoided calls. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/harnessmm-recursive-self-improvement-for-harness-token-efficiency-2610.32813), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2114732037827662278) |
| 4) **EMMA** - Methods that evolve multi-agent systems often follow the backbone model's habits instead of the observed failures, so one backbone accumulates skills while another spawns agents. Researchers from UC San Diego and Amazon present EMMA, which stores a multi-agent system's configuration as episodic memory and restricts how that memory can change. <br>● Three memory levels: A controller plans each task one step at a time and draws on routing memory. Specialist agents inherit a generalist prompt, and skills hold short per-step procedures, all as text run by one fixed LLM.  <br>● Scoped updates: After a failure, a reflector assigns the fix to the scope that owns it and proposes one edit there. Guards that do not depend on the backbone keep every accepted edit scoped, incremental, and bounded.  <br>● Results: Across KramaBench, AppWorld, and BBEH with five backbones, EMMA has the best overall performance on every benchmark against ACE, GEPA, EvoMAS, and ADAS, up to 32.5% over the strongest baseline. It ranks first in 17 of 20 settings and uses 17 to 38% fewer training executions than the most efficient baseline.  <br>● Why it matters: Multi-agent search baselines fall sharply on open-weight models. On AppWorld challenge, EvoMAS scores 89.0 with Opus 5 and 7.2 with GLM-5, while EMMA's weakest backbone still reaches 61.2. The authors note that accepted edits persist even when a task stays unsolved, so ineffective entries may remain. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/emma-structured-episodic-memory-for-multi-agent-workflow-synthesis-2610.35966), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2115438189901181296) |
| 5) **A Heart Means Recursion** - Researchers from Google DeepMind and Stanford find that simple visual symbols can change how vision-language models behave on unrelated tasks, and that the models do not report the influence. With no image, Claude Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.4 always write iterative code for inorder binary tree traversal. <br>● Visual steering: A red heart in context moves Claude Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.4 to recursion in 100% of samples, and a yellow checkmark moves Gemini 3.1 Pro to 90%. The effect matches or approaches directly instructing the model to use recursion.  <br>● Images over captions: Text captions of the same symbols do not reproduce the pattern, and random or naturalistic images steer less. The same 288 colored shapes also shift writing style and answers to simple preference questions.  <br>● Hidden influence: For GPT-5.4, 74.7% of thinking traces misattribute the symbol's role. When images are inserted silently, Claude Opus 4.7 still reaches a full switch to recursion, and GPT-5.4's traces never mention the image.  <br>● Why it matters: When models send images to each other, they choose shapes such as spirals and arrows that they and humans find intuitive, and these often fail to steer the receiver. Agents that read screenshots can change behavior in ways the reasoning trace will not show, although the tasks in this study are synthetic. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/a-heart-means-recursion-exploring-visual-meaning-and-communication-in-vlms-2610.36449), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2115474180078789094) |
| 6) **Verification in Agent Harnesses** - Harness builders have little evidence on where verification effort pays off. Researchers from Alibaba and Cornell compare 17 designs across seven frontier coding models on Terminal-Bench and SWE-bench Verified, spanning initial instructions, execution-time reminders, completion gates, and independent review. They also introduce VisualBench for expert evaluation of visual and interactive artifacts. <br>● Instructions and reminders: Gains are modest and depend on the model. System-role instructions add 3.3 points on Terminal-Bench and 3.9 on SWE-bench, and system-role reminders add 3.1 to 3.8 points.  <br>● Completion gates: Early user rejection gives the largest gate gains at 6.0 and 6.2 points, but rejection designs cost 1.60x to 1.76x baseline on Terminal-Bench and 1.48x to 1.56x on SWE-bench. The authors recommend avoiding completion-time self-checking.  <br>● Independent review: A separate critic adds 5.9 to 9.5 points on Terminal-Bench at 1.13x to 1.25x baseline cost, and 3.9 to 6.6 points on SWE-bench at 1.00x to 1.04x. Extra review rounds or critics add up to 3.5 more points on Terminal-Bench.  <br>● Why it matters: The critic designs also lead on VisualBench, with net preferences of 13.2 to 23.8 points over the baseline, and reviewing the changes beats reviewing the trajectory there. Teams adding one verification step to a harness have a measured case for a separate reviewer. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/rethinking-verification-in-agent-harnesses-2610.34577), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2115101219119325606) |
| 7) **EmbeddingGemma 2** - Multimodal embedding models adapted from large language models typically carry several billion parameters, which makes them expensive for on-device and latency-sensitive work. Google presents EmbeddingGemma 2, an open 744M model that maps text, images, audio, video, and mixed inputs into one vector space. <br>● Modular design: A 167M vision encoder and a 305M audio encoder feed a 271M bidirectional transformer, so teams load only what they need. It runs at 271M for text, 439M as a vision-language model, and 577M as an audio-language model.  <br>● Training recipe: The model builds on the Gemma 4 family and learns from Gemini Embedding 2. Training runs in three stages, separating text-only from multimodal training and distillation from contrastive objectives.  <br>● Results: Across seven benchmark suites covering text, code, image, video, document, speech, and sound, it sets a new Pareto frontier, beating specialist models and models two to twelve times larger. MTEB Multilingual v2 reaches 61.36 and MTEB Code rises from 68.76 to 78.68 over its predecessor, while MTEB English v2 falls from 69.67 to 68.46.  <br>● Why it matters: The int4 version loses about 0.4 points on MTEB Multilingual, and teams can also lower the vision token budget or truncate embedding dimensions. That makes it a practical on-device option for agent memory and retrieval over mixed media. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/embeddinggemma-2-unified-and-efficient-multimodal-representations-2610.37364), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2115439702572716129) |
| 8) **AgentBaton** - Researchers from NVIDIA, HKUST, and UIUC study one-time handoffs from an expensive capable model to a cheaper one within a long-horizon agent task, and their AgentBaton detector learns when to make the switch from patterns in task progress. Across 11 model pairs on DeepSWE v1.1 and Terminal-Bench 2.1, it cuts API cost by 65.9% on average while largely preserving accuracy, with gains of up to 8.8 points over capable-only runs. The cost figures assign zero cost to locally deployable models, and handoffs in the other direction save little or nothing. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/agentbaton-efficient-model-collaboration-in-agentic-tasks-2610.35959), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2115888920860958961) |
| 9) **Agentic Backdoors** - Researchers from the Anthropic Fellows Program, Anthropic, UC Berkeley, and UIUC show that poisoning 0.1% of the pretraining corpus can plant a remote code execution backdoor that survives a clean post-training pipeline into a CLI agent. On a 4B model, an ordinary file path in the user's request reaches a Pass@32 attack success rate of 92.7%, while clean prompts produce the payload 0.0% of the time. Attack success rises with model size, and at a 0.0005% poison rate an injected trigger still fired on 1.4% of prompts in a single-seed run. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/agentic-backdoors-via-pretraining-poisoning-2610.37741), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2116183621157736540) |
| 10) **System One Rerankers** - Researchers from the University of Passau and IT:U test whether decision fine-tuning makes a better reranker than plain reranker fine-tuning, using six Qwen backbones from 0.6B to 27B on ten TREC DL and BEIR collections. Reranker fine-tuning wins by 5 to 29 nDCG@10 points from 0.6B to 9B and the two tie at 27B, while decision fine-tuning adds nothing over the untuned model up to 9B. The calibration advantage of decision models depends on how the question is worded, and it does not make up for a worse ranking. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/system-one-models-are-not-better-rerankers-2610.32318), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2115176716947833003) |

## Top AI Papers of the Week (October 19 - October 25) - 2026
| **Paper**  | **Links** |
| ------------- | ------------- |
| 1) **Object-Oriented Harness** - An agent harness is the executable code that sets the prompts, tools, and control flow around a model, and a single global harness can struggle when tasks need different execution strategies. Researchers from Google Cloud AI Research and the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign introduce OO-Harness, which evolves a hierarchy of specialized harness classes using object-oriented design. <br>● Class hierarchy: During training, an evolution agent reads execution records and builds a family of specialized harnesses. Each one is a subclass of the base harness or of an existing specialized harness, and it overrides only the methods its task cluster needs.  <br>● Lessons and routing: Design lessons record which changes helped and which ones failed. At test time, a router reads the task and the base run, then picks a subclass or keeps the base result, using routing lessons gathered during training.  <br>● Results: With the same Gemini 3.5 Flash executor, AutomationBench rises from 61.6% to 77.4%, Workspace-Bench from 25.5% to 39.5%, and strict pass on Toolathlon-GYM from 31.2% to 37.5%. OO-Harness beats Meta-Harness on all three benchmarks, while spending extra inference on best-of-K sampling or self-refinement does not reliably help.  <br>● Why it matters: Inheritance itself adds value. On Workspace-Bench, harnesses built as subclasses score 4.96 points higher than the same evolved code copied into flat classes, which gives teams building harnesses for mixed workloads a concrete reason to specialize instead of growing one global harness. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/object-oriented-harness-evolving-agent-harnesses-through-object-oriented-design-2610.28380), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2112911038098317422) |
| 2) **Gemini Agents Beat Minecraft** - Researchers from Google DeepMind placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world with one goal, defeating the Ender Dragon. They report what they believe is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, using only the standard Mineflayer library with no pre-scouted seeds or game-specific tools written by humans. <br>● Setup: Each agent controlled one player through Mineflayer and coordinated with its teammates through in-game chat and a shared memory. No one assigned roles or wrote game logic, and the agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves.  <br>● The run: Starting from a random world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs, died 605 times, and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes. Researchers watched on a live dashboard and never needed to intervene.  <br>● Team strategies: The agents divided the work, shared what they learned, and recovered each other's items after deaths. A fifth Observer agent watched the session for cheating or specification gaming and found none.  <br>● Why it matters: Winning required keeping one plan coherent across thousands of dependent steps for more than a day, the same long-horizon coding and coordination that real software and enterprise tasks demand. The authors are clear about the limits, which include a single run, Mineflayer exposing hidden game state, Easy difficulty, and the model's prior knowledge of Minecraft. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/a-team-of-gemini-4-argon-agents-beats-minecraft-2610.22319), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2111093058024755365) |
| 3) **DiscoveryRSI** - Scientific discovery depends on turning worthwhile hypotheses into practical investigations, and learning from that evolving research process is hard. Researchers from PhAI Labs, CUHK, Stanford, Harvard Medical School, and colleagues present DiscoveryRSI, which applies recursive self-improvement to hypothesis generation across four life-science domains. <br>● Hypothesis trees: The system grows trees of hypotheses with multi-agent evolutionary search and compiles the selected investigations into executable Harbor tasks. The authors define a valuable hypothesis as an evidence-grounded claim about a consequential uncertainty, paired with a feasible investigation, explicit decision criteria, and alternative explanations.  <br>● Three learning mechanisms: Hypothesis credit optimization attributes scientific assessments to the research contributions behind them. Harness replay finds productive strategies and recurring failures in the research workflow, and rediscovery applies accepted improvements to new investigations.  <br>● Results: Automated hypothesis quality rises from 4.86 for the base model to 5.39 after two rounds of self-improvement, with the largest gains in falsifiability and data resolvability. The 27B model's hypotheses match research published after its training cutoff 6.3% of the time, compared with 2.5% for GPT-6 and 2.3% for Claude Opus 5.  <br>● Why it matters: Preliminary expert review rates DiscoveryRSI-27B above GPT-6 Luna on hypothesis importance and on whether claims are plausible and evidence-bounded. The paper also releases DFM-Bench for scoring hypotheses, revisions, and whole research runs. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/discoveryrsi-recursively-learning-to-discover-valuable-hypotheses-2610.23839), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2112295238664696237) |
| 4) **Cache Engineering for Agents** - As agent trajectories grow, KV cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Context engineering trims or summarizes history, but changing the trajectory prefix invalidates the cache after it, and the retained context has to be prefilled again. Researchers from Tsinghua University and CUHK propose cache engineering, where the agent controls its active KV cache directly. <br>● Two cache operations: A drop operation excludes selected cache entries from future attention, and a repos operation adjusts the position encodings of the keys that remain. The trajectory text stays unchanged, so nothing needs to be prefilled again.  <br>● AKV serving engine: Identical token sequences can produce different cache states under different sequences of operations, so prefix matching is not enough for correct cache reuse. AKV exposes a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs and manages stateful reuse, eviction, and restoration internally.  <br>● Results: A rolling drop of old tool responses scores 1.7 points higher on average and costs 27.7% less than threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it reaches up to 1.2x output throughput and 1.5x faster P95 latency.  <br>● Why it matters: Recursive self-improvement of cache policies cuts cost by another 7.1% over expert design. Cost control for long-running agents can happen at the cache layer, below the prompt, and the code is open source. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/cache-engineering-for-agents-2610.28577), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2112968165680640143) |
| 5) **JevAgentHarness** - Agent harnesses call a large LLM at every step, which makes long tasks slow and costly, yet most of those calls end in a multiple-choice decision such as which tool to call next. Researchers from UIUC, Princeton, and Google study how a harness can hand these choices to a decision model like Jev with little loss of success. <br>● AgentDecisionBench: The benchmark tests decision models from isolated questions up to control of a running agent. The model most accurate on isolated questions solved only 69.5% of agent tasks, and delegating tool choices without a fitted confidence threshold lowered success by 19.0 points.  <br>● Fitted thresholds: JevAgentHarness sends each routine choice to the decision model and uses its answer only when confidence clears a threshold fitted on the agent's own trajectories. Below the threshold, the LLM decides. With fitted thresholds, tool and argument cascades cut cost by 14.2% without changing the outcome of any of 500 tasks.  <br>● AgentDecision-4B: Existing decision models never see the states an agent reaches after its own mistakes, so the authors train an open 4B model on those states with Jev's answers as labels. On tasks unlike its training data, the agent solved 91.5% with AgentDecision-4B and 82.0% with Jev.  <br>● Why it matters: On tasks like their training data, 4B models trained this way made agents 81.1% to 89.0% cheaper and up to 2.5x faster while staying within 3 points of task success. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/jevagentharness-benchmarking-deploying-and-training-decision-models-that-share-c-2610.21412), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2111496216567742892) |
| 6) **Evolving Agent Interfaces** - Computer-use agents act through interfaces that people design by hand, so an agent may type a spreadsheet cell by cell when one line of code would do. Researchers from Simular introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds its own interfaces from task experience. <br>● Meta-agent: A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, with no success labels, and builds and revises interfaces along with notes on when to use each one. Both live in a repository the task agent draws from.  <br>● Population-based selection: Evolution keeps the repositories that perform best on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, which favors changes that carry over to new tasks.  <br>● Results: With Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved interfaces, Agent S4 reaches 21.3% on OSWorld V2, 3.8x the same model with fixed interfaces and a new state of the art for open-source computer-use agents. That is above GPT-5.5 at 13.0% and Claude Opus 4.8 at 20.6%, at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their costs.  <br>● Why it matters: On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces raise success from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. The authors argue that interface design should be an agent capability, a source of improvement that complements better models and harnesses, and the code is open source. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/evolving-agent-interfaces-2610.28601), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2112914561590546695) |
| 7) **Self-Evolving Continual Learners** - Deployed agents face a steady arrival of new knowledge, tools, and workflows, and they are expected to learn new tasks without forgetting earlier ones. Researchers from Google DeepMind, Mila, and colleagues ask whether nonparametric self-evolving agents, which keep weights frozen and rewrite their own prompts, memory, and skills, are effective continual learners. <br>● Drifting streams: The study builds four task streams from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld that shift in topic, workflow, or domain. It evaluates ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness, covering both experience retrieval and self-modification.  <br>● Plasticity costs stability: Self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, but higher plasticity consistently comes with more forgetting. Across all 12 method and scenario pairs, plasticity and stability show a correlation of r = -0.72.  <br>● Two failure mechanisms: Self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, which interferes with unseen tasks and overwrites older knowledge as the stream shifts. Similarity-based memory retrieval keeps updates more local, but it still retrieves memories from other domains and suffers collisions when similar queries need conflicting workflows.  <br>● Why it matters: The authors conclude that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners. Static benchmarks hide both problems, so teams building self-improving agents should test them on streams that change over time. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/are-self-evolving-agents-continual-learners-2610.25858), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2112728082042015769) |
| 8) **Terminal-RSI** - Researchers from Tencent train terminal agents by alternating two phases over five stages starting from Qwen3.8-27B, fine-tuning on rewritten versions of verified trajectories and then running PPO with a critic inherited from the previous stage. The four-suite Terminal-Bench average rises from 20.68% to 50.58%, with 86.52% on Terminal-Bench 2.1 and 79.00% on Terminal-Bench Hard. A reward audit found that under a dense progress reward, failed runs collected 50.09% of the reward, and separating strict success from partial credit cut that share to 28.72%. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/terminal-rsi-recursive-self-improving-terminal-agents-via-recursive-self-rewrite-2610.22192), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2111487480281416004) |
| 9) **Transcoder Features Are Neurons** - Researchers from Anthropic and Georgia Tech find that many active transcoder features in late layers are close copies of single MLP neurons, measured across 13 open-source transcoders. These neuron-aligned features are the only ones with a stable link to the underlying MLP, while the other features vary more between training runs, do not match the MLP's Jacobian, and move less from their random initialization. The authors conclude that MLP neurons are a stronger basis for interpretability than commonly assumed, and that the non-neuron features need better validation before they are used to explain model behavior. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/comparing-sparsified-mlps-and-transcoders-many-transcoder-features-are-neurons-i-2610.24521), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2112619619865579572) |
| 10) **MemoryTax** - This short study runs four memory methods (full context, BM25, Mem0, Zep) and three coding agents (Pi, Codex, Claude Code) on one 419-turn LoCoMo conversation with three models, recording the cost of every rollout. Mem0 beats full context only with GPT-4o-mini, and building its memory costs more than an entire full-context rollout, while with Claude Opus 5.5 the Pi coding agent searching the raw history scores 99% for under half the cost of full context. Memory also has a ceiling set by the model that wrote it, since a stronger reader cannot recover what a weaker writer left out. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/memorytax-the-bitter-lesson-of-agent-memory-2610.29823), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2113272925558305191) |

## Top AI Papers of the Week (October 12 - October 18) - 2026
| **Paper**  | **Links** |
| ------------- | ------------- |
| 1) **Quantum Circuit Discovery** - Many quantum algorithms spend most of their resources on a small set of computational primitives, and compiling those primitives into quantum circuits is hard because the computation must be reversible. Researchers from Anthropic, Harvard, and QuEra Computing pair an LLM that proposes circuit designs with an independent evaluator that verifies and scores every candidate. <br>● Evolutionary search: The search runs over circuit-generating programs with OpenEvolve, using Claude Mythos 5 to propose architectures and program rewrites. Each search starts from a hand-written task specification and a blank program, and the best designs seed the next round.  <br>● Verified circuits: The evaluator checks each circuit for correctness before scoring it by Toffoli count and peak qubit count. Every reported gain is a circuit that passed this check.  <br>● Results: On seven of eight tasks spanning numerical computation, chemistry, optimization, quantum simulation, and number theory, the discovered circuits use fewer Toffoli gates and fewer qubits than the best-known designs, cutting the combined cost by 4.1x to 39x. For a Reed-Solomon decoder, the search switched to the Berlekamp-Massey recurrence and lowered the Toffoli count from 5.1 million to 440,000.  <br>● Why it matters: On the eighth task, the search found a new reversible architecture for prime-field modular inversion that makes divsteps, a classical algorithm previously impractical for reversible integer arithmetic, usable and improves the Toffoli-qubit trade-off. The authors suggest that some apparent costs of quantum arithmetic reflect limits of existing reversible constructions rather than of the algorithms themselves. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/discovering-state-of-the-art-quantum-circuits-with-large-language-models-2610.22367), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2111155277886316847) |
| 2) **LongHarness** - Agents that reason over document collections larger than their context window usually keep retrieved evidence and their evolving conclusions mixed into one growing interaction history. Researchers from Shanghai AI Laboratory, Fudan University, and colleagues present LongHarness, a model-agnostic harness for corpora of 100M tokens and more that keeps evidence and conclusions as explicit state. <br>● Two failure modes: The authors identify unbounded exploration, where retrieval keeps expanding the history without closing the information gap that matters, and evidence-state drift, where earlier evidence or later corrections stop reaching the current conclusion.  <br>● Evidence-Conclusion Coevolution: Open conclusions decide what to retrieve next, and each new piece of evidence strengthens or revises the conclusions that asked for it. An evidence lifecycle step keeps accumulated evidence current and traceable throughout the interaction.  <br>● Same model, much higher scores: With GLM-5.3-Flash, exact match on OfficeQA Pro rises from 15.8% to 60.2% on V1 and from 4.4% to 61.1% on V2 compared with direct reasoning. LongHarness beats Claude Code by 12.0 points on V1 and Codex by 18.9 points on V2, and Qwen3.8-Flash reaches 61.7% on V1.  <br>● Why it matters: The gains come from the harness, with the backbone held fixed. Teams building agents for long-context work over large internal corpora can get large improvements by managing evidence explicitly instead of relying on a growing chat history. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/longharness-advancing-agentic-reasoning-over-100m-token-corpora-via-stateful-evi-2610.13075), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2110083302728323346) |
| 3) **Hybrid Reasoning Agents** - Today's LLM agents write out every reasoning step as text before they search, run code, or take an action, and those steps cost tokens and time. Researchers from Stanford, Together AI, and colleagues introduce Hybrid Reasoning Agents, which reason internally in latent space and switch to text only to call a tool or give an answer. <br>● HybridLink: A lightweight module moves the model between latent and text modes within a single generation process. A continuous working memory keeps both kinds of context across turns, tool calls stay in plain text, and the latent steps can still be inspected.  <br>● Two-stage training: HybridSFT first teaches the backbone to work with HybridLink during tool-integrated generation. HybridRL then teaches the model when to think in latent space and when to act through text.  <br>● Results: Across 3 model scales and 11 benchmarks in math, science, medicine, search, and agentic reasoning, it improves over frontier LLM agents and agentic frameworks, both text-based and latent, by 10.4% on average. The 14B model beats the strongest agentic baseline by 17.4% on agentic reasoning tasks.  <br>● Why it matters: The agents use 35.5% to 54.7% fewer tokens, run 1.8x to 2.6x faster, and cost 2.7x to 3.7x less. Latent reasoning has mostly been studied without tools, and this work shows a way to combine its efficiency with standard tool use. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/hybrid-reasoning-agents-2610.18861), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2110488828062675096) |
| 4) **Harness^2** - Recursive self-improvement of agent harnesses usually depends on a verifier, and open-world tasks are new at test time, so no ground-truth checker exists. Researchers from Google Cloud AI Research and Northwestern University introduce Harness^2, a plug-and-play framework that improves the harness at test time through two connected levels of recursion. <br>● Task-level recursion: A contrastive Propose-Probe-Compose step proposes several edits to the harness for the current task, tests them against each other, and keeps the combination that works best. This guides edits reliably without a ground-truth verifier.  <br>● Harnessing-level recursion: Lessons from those edits update a second, continual improvement harness that the agent carries to future tasks, so improvements carry over instead of staying task-specific.  <br>● Results: A single step improves GLM-5 and Gemini 3.5 Flash over their base harnesses in every configuration tested, with gains of up to 11.5 points on WorkBuddy-Bench, 4.5 points on JobBench, and 11.8 points on LAB. Running five candidate edits in parallel adds another 2.9 to 3.9 points on WorkBuddy-Bench.  <br>● Why it matters: ReasoningBank, a memory baseline, fell up to 6.6 points below the base harness on coding tasks in the same comparison. For teams evolving their own harnesses, the paper shows that test-time harness edits can scale with more recursion budget even when no verifier is available. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/harness-2-recursive-agent-harnessing-for-an-open-world-2610.14224), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2110755132548038788) |
| 5) **Scaling Discovery Requires Forgetting** - Automated research with coding agents can now run for days on a single problem, yet the search often stops improving early. Researchers from UC Berkeley, Stanford, and Bespoke Labs show that the fix is to make the agents forget. <br>● The plateau: Letting one agent iterate longer, running agents in parallel, and letting parallel agents share candidates all plateau as history accumulates. Each agent anchors on its own past attempts and makes fewer and smaller edits, so extra budget brings little improvement.  <br>● Forgetting: The method periodically discards every agent's session and the candidate history, then restarts the team from the best candidate found so far. The authors implement this in a team of agents that communicate with each other.  <br>● Results: Across four tasks in heuristic programming, kernel optimization, and model training, forgetting gives up to 15% over parallel agents and up to 39% over a single agent with the same budget. On Anthropic's VLIW kernel take-home, the team with forgetting reaches 1,077 cycles, against 1,172 for four parallel agents and 1,262 for one agent.  <br>● Why it matters: Most research-agent designs try to keep as much history as possible. This paper suggests that, for open-ended discovery, what an agent is allowed to remember matters as much as how much compute it gets. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/scaling-discovery-requires-forgetting-2610.20563), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2110911109964386460) |
| 6) **ReHarness** - Agent memory harnesses usually only write forward, so a lesson goes into the bank and is never checked against whether it helped. Researchers from MIT, Meta, and Yale propose ReHarness, a recurrent harness for agentic continual learning that adds backward revision to the usual forward read and write. <br>● Backward revision: After each task, ReHarness uses the finished trajectory as feedback on the specific reflections that were retrieved for it and rewrites them. Feedback flows backward through the textual harness state, much like a recurrent update.  <br>● Strictly online: The method runs under a streaming protocol with no replay buffer or validation set. Add and delete operations alone keep a reflection bank tidy but ignore downstream outcomes, while ReHarness corrects lessons based on how they performed.  <br>● Results: Across three continual-learning benchmarks with 16 tasks and three backbones, ReHarness beats raw-context, memory, reflection, and prompt-optimization baselines. The gains stay significant after multiple-comparison correction.  <br>● Why it matters: Agents running over long interaction streams face distribution shifts, and stored lessons can become overgeneralized or outdated. The analysis shows that ReHarness produces source-grounded revisions that fix such lessons and improves both stability and plasticity. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/recurrent-harness-for-agentic-continual-learning-2610.12668), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2110017231778373722) |
| 7) **The Agentic Scientist** - Fully autonomous research agents tend to have a narrow range of ideas and hill-climb myopically. Researchers from Sakana AI introduce The Agentic Scientist, an end-to-end distributed pipeline that lets human researchers run computational research alongside modern coding agents. <br>● Staged pipeline: The system splits a project into task drafting, ideation, planning, sandboxed experiments, baselines and ablations, analysis, and report writing. Each stage runs on a coding agent that verifies its own work.  <br>● Meta-controller: A controller manages many research agents in parallel and checks every stage against a rubric. The same orchestration layer can be reused across domains with different execution environments, coding harnesses, and evaluation metrics.  <br>● Accepted paper: Used as a co-scientist, a development version of the system helped write a paper that was accepted at ALIFE 2026 as a main-conference talk, after human verification and edits. The system can also run fully autonomously, and the authors apply it to open machine learning problems across several domains of computational research.  <br>● Why it matters: The ablations show that reliable scientific autonomy depends on the cross-agent orchestration layer as well as on the base model. It is a useful reference for anyone building multi-agent systems around coding agents. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/the-agentic-scientist-orchestrating-coding-agents-for-automated-scientific-co-di-2610.18933), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2110733945323368822) |
| 8) **GitSwarm** - Researchers from Meta Superintelligence Labs and colleagues build GitSwarm, where hundreds of asynchronous agents share one Git repository and each contribution is a commit that records which earlier commits, on any branch, it builds on, with no central planner assigning work. With GPT-5.5 Codex workers it solves all 30 IMOProofBench-Advanced problems in one run and scores 79.4% on ProgramBench against 65.1% for a single Codex agent at a similar budget, rising from 73.1% at 100 workers to 79.4% at 500. Later workers build on 94.7% of published contributions, and 70.1% of failed research commits are cited by later accepted work. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/gitswarm-decentralized-compounding-inference-2610.04862), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2109287161199812802) |
| 9) **LoGRA** - NVIDIA researchers present LoGRA, an RL post-training method that updates all of a model's weights while using less memory than LoRA, by compressing each weight gradient into a low-rank sketch that is redrawn every step and adding predicted-KL step control to keep updates within a budget. At 1.5B it averages 7.2 GiB of training memory against 13.2 GiB for rank-256 LoRA with similar Pass@4, and at 7B it cuts average training memory per GPU by 45.7% with peak accuracy close to dense Adam. It also trains a 27B model for more than 1,100 steps on a single eight-GPU node, where dense Adam runs out of memory. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/logra-scaling-llm-reinforcement-learning-with-low-rank-gradient-sketches-2610.06647), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2109771098120450299) |
| 10) **AutoEnvScaling** - Researchers from Microsoft and collaborators hand RL environment design to a proposer agent that works in a terminal, studies the rollouts of the model being trained, and builds new environments from them, with each round of rollouts guiding the next. Building environments in a terminal raises the share of valid environments up to 25 times over prompt-only generation at up to 75% lower cost per valid environment. With Qwen3.6-35B-A3B acting as both proposer and solver, Terminal-Bench 2.1 rises from 41.1 to 53.3, and the pipeline also generates environments for software engineering, computer use, and GPU kernel work. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/autoenvscaling-automating-the-data-flywheel-with-terminal-agents-2610.14163), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2110467939057168849) |

## Top AI Papers of the Week (October 5 - October 11) - 2026
| **Paper**  | **Links** |
| ------------- | ------------- |
| 1) **Harness Learning** - An agent is defined by its model and its harness, the program that organizes model calls, tool use, and information flow. Researchers from CMU train a proposer model to revise that harness from execution feedback, and the solver model's weights never change. <br>● Edits as updates: The authors frame harness adaptation as meta-learning over executable programs, where each harness revision plays the role of a weight update in gradient-based adaptation. The proposer reads a task, the current harness, and an execution report, then writes a code edit to the harness.  <br>● RL on harness scores: The proposer is trained with reinforcement learning, and the reward is the task score of the revised harness. At test time it refines the harness on a new task from successive executions, with no parameter update.  <br>● Small proposer, broad transfer: A trained 4B proposer beats its 35B teacher at single-step revision on Reasoning Gym, including task families it never saw in training. A proposer trained on HotpotQA keeps improving harnesses on MuSiQue and 2WikiMultihopQA, and policies trained on single revisions continue improving harnesses over multiple rounds.  <br>● Why it matters: Improving agents by editing harness code instead of weights is becoming its own AI engineering skill, and this work shows the skill can be learned and transferred to unseen tasks. It points toward agents that turn accumulated experience into general improvements without retraining the underlying model. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/harness-learning-enables-generalizable-test-time-adaptation-2609.35738), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2106529236068819394) |
| 2) **CorpusMap** - Agents that search large document collections usually see the corpus as a flat set of files, so a relevant document gives no hint of how it connects to others. Researchers from Microsoft and colleagues introduce CorpusMap, a navigation layer built around the entities that recur across a collection. <br>● Entity pages: CorpusMap resolves mentions of the same entity across documents in advance and gives each recurring entity a page that aggregates what is known about it and links to every document that refers to it. The original documents stay in place.  <br>● Built once, reused per query: The agent reads a document, follows an entity to related documents, and avoids searching for the same evidence again. The links are built offline, so they are shared across queries instead of rediscovered at inference time. The map can be built without LLM calls and updated as new documents arrive.  <br>● Better answers, fewer tokens: Across 7 models and three benchmarks, answer quality rises 6.4 to 11.7 points over raw-corpus agentic search while input tokens drop 34% to 57%. CorpusMap also beats an LLM Wiki layer and three other navigation layers.  <br>● Why it matters: Enterprise questions often need evidence spread across emails, reports, and contracts in different folders. A precomputed entity graph gives agents structure to follow, which reduces both missed evidence and repeated searches. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/follow-the-entities-a-corpus-map-for-agentic-search-2609.37226), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2107146326639358188) |
| 3) **VeriHarness** - A common practice with long-horizon agents is to sample several rollouts and trust the answers they agree on. Researchers from Google show that agreement can hide shared errors while disagreement often points to the correct alternative, and they build VeriHarness to verify rollouts with the same base model. <br>● Verifier from the generator's model: VeriHarness gives the model the generator uses a workspace, evidence tools, and reusable verification skills. It needs no reference answers or grading rubrics at test time.  <br>● Two checks: A disagreement resolver checks competing claims against environment evidence. A consensus challenger tests claims that every rollout shares and looks for requirements they all missed. Their findings guide which artifact to select and how to revise it.  <br>● Results: Across five long-horizon workspace benchmarks and two frontier models, VeriHarness reaches the highest selection scores among the baselines tested. With evidence-backed revision, it adds 6.2 points over a single rollout with Gemini 3.5 Flash and 6.4 points with Claude Opus 4.8. Its verification skills also improve from failure feedback.  <br>● Why it matters: Majority voting discards the minority answer, which is sometimes the correct one. The authors release about 26,000 rollouts across all five benchmarks and both models, produced at a cost of over $100,000, to support further work on agentic verification. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/veriharness-scaling-agentic-verification-for-long-horizon-tasks-2610.00972), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2106700905051746803) |
| 4) **HERMES** - Software engineering agents on long-horizon tasks keep reconstructing program state spread across source files, configurations, tests, and dependencies, which leads to long interaction histories and context explosion. HERMES is a harness that turns repository components into active participants in the task. <br>● Dev-Primitives: Each repository component is paired with a resident LLM that knows its own implementation and dependencies. The component gets an agent-native interface for natural-language reasoning, communication with other components, and localized self-modification.  <br>● Activation and diagnosis: A dependency-aware step decides which components to activate for a task, and a diagnosis step maps test failures and execution evidence back to the components that need changes.  <br>● Same model, different harness: On whole-repository migration, GPT-5.6 Sol goes from 6.5% to 31.0% when Codex is replaced with HERMES, at the same model and effort setting. Across four software engineering benchmarks, HERMES beats matched baseline harnesses by 12.4% on average.  <br>● Why it matters: With strong activation and diagnosis models, HERMES running Qwen3-8B Dev-Primitives stays within 4.5% of an all-GPT-5.6 Sol configuration on all four benchmarks and cuts inference cost on Terminal-Bench 4.0 by 26.2%. Most of the migration gain comes from the harness, so a harness built around your own codebase is worth the engineering effort. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/harness-engineering-for-software-engineering-via-modular-executable-dev-primitiv-2610.07832), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2108360049848717588) |
| 5) **MASS** - Recursive self-improvement on open-ended tasks such as research runs into a supervision problem, because the outputs can exceed what even human experts can reliably assess and there is no external checker. Researchers from Sakana AI propose Multi-Agent Self-Supervision (MASS), where a single base model acts as the agent, the evaluator, and the optimizer. <br>● Workflows as the search space: The base model proposes multi-agent workflows, runs them, and grades the results. An evolutionary search constrained by structural guardrails keeps the best-scoring workflows and settles on distinct roles and information routing for each task.  <br>● Alternating cycles: Each cycle pairs that workflow optimization with supervised fine-tuning on the model's own multi-agent traces. The improved model then starts the next cycle as a better optimizer and grader, so the loop can keep bootstrapping the model's capabilities.  <br>● Results: Two MASS cycles on Qwen3.6-27B give 1.2 to 1.6x higher performance per output token on four open-ended public benchmarks. A student trained on multi-agent traces also beats a single-agent student trained on 1.4x more tokens.  <br>● Why it matters: Self-improvement loops usually depend on an external verifier, so open-ended tasks with no checker are left out. MASS shows that one model can supply its own supervision signal by learning orchestration and bounded subagent execution from multi-agent trajectories. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/recursive-self-improvement-through-multi-agent-self-supervision-2610.12176), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2108934334589857850) |
| 6) **Sharpening Tax** - A common hypothesis holds that RL post-training only sharpens behaviors the base model already has, raising single-shot accuracy at the cost of solution coverage. Researchers from Meta Superintelligence Labs test whether this holds for agentic tasks, where multi-turn tool use might need capabilities learned during post-training. <br>● Base models as agents: Pre-trained LLMs with a light inference harness work as capable agents. Post-trained models win on pass@1, but at large K the base models often solve tasks their post-trained versions never solve on BFCL v4 multi-turn, ACEBench, and WebShop.  <br>● Why coverage drops: Post-training pushes each task toward always solved or never solved. Consistency and sampling efficiency go up, and coverage goes down.  <br>● Measuring the cost: The authors propose the Sharpening Tax, a metric for the test-time scalability lost after post-training. Across 42 base and post-trained cases from four model families and three benchmarks, the tax shows up in most settings, grows with model size, and can be estimated from a few rollouts.  <br>● Why it matters: Their fix, posterior-tempered group sampling (PTGS), sets the sampling temperature per prompt from its estimated difficulty during RL. It pays a smaller tax than a fixed-temperature baseline and also raises pass@1. If your agent relies on repeated sampling at test time, a base model may cover tasks that your post-trained model has lost. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/sharpening-tax-in-post-training-2610.01509), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2106418508259377411) |
| 7) **VERA** - Long-horizon agents need environments that can be resumed at any stage and scored from observable evidence, yet most environments score only the final outcome. Researchers from NVIDIA present VERA, which builds such environments at scale and uses them to update both the model and its harness. <br>● Verifiable environments from trajectories: VERA turns initial trajectories into more than 9,000 restartable sandboxes. An agent writes rubrics and executable checks, a judge verifies each sandbox, and only environments that run and can be scored from observable evidence enter the training bank.  <br>● Two update paths: VERA alternates between training the model with rubric rewards and editing the harness skills. A harness edit is kept only if it passes self-tests and adds at least 5 points on the development set, and a model checkpoint is rejected if its score drops by more than 20%.  <br>● Results: At 9B, the co-evolved agent beats the strongest single-axis baseline by 10.3 points on AutoCoWorkBench and 13.0 points on AutoMedBench. At 27B, it scores 71.6 on AutoCoWorkBench and 80.7 on AutoMedBench, transfers to unseen workflows, and keeps its general capabilities.  <br>● Why it matters: Training the harness alongside the model is a big part of owning the intelligence stack, and many frontier AI companies have started doing it. The open-source corpus of environments gives others a starting point for this kind of co-evolution on long-horizon work. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/vera-scaling-verifiable-environments-for-agentic-co-evolution-2610.05923), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2107849444897243141) |
| 8) **Agent Plasticity** - Researchers from Meta Superintelligence Labs introduce agent plasticity, a measure of how efficiently an agent converts experience into gains on held-out tasks per dollar spent on learning, with model weights frozen and each run starting from a fresh context while inheriting artifacts written by earlier runs. The model with the best final score is often a different model from the one that learns most efficiently. In chess, Go, and Hex, Claude Fable 5 reaches the highest final score while GPT-5.6 Sol gains the most per dollar, and in NetHack only Claude Opus 5.5 improves significantly, by 66 normalized points for about $1,073 of learning. Slow learners often ignore artifacts they already wrote, while faster learners reuse their artifacts and still fail when an artifact is low quality. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/agent-plasticity-measuring-self-improvement-through-experience-2610.08902), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2108580251127439710) |
| 9) **Continuous Memory Machines** - Sakana AI's Continuous Memory Machine gives a recurrent model two matrix-valued memories, a short-term store that tracks recent neuron activity and a long-term store for information needed later, with a Transformer reading and writing both at every step. Built on the Continuous Thought Machine, it beats LSTM, DNC, RMC, and CTM baselines on copy, associative recall, sorting, few-shot regression, and maze solving, and it generalizes to longer inputs than earlier memory-augmented networks. Its attention maps show it uses long-term memory for algorithmic and in-context tasks and skips it when a task does not need it. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/continuous-memory-machines-2610.07907), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2108359294609768715) |
| 10) **MIMESIS** - Agent RL setups usually let an assistant LLM play the user, which makes the simulated user overly cooperative and explicit, and a fixed GPT-5.5 agent finds tau-bench tasks easier with these users than with real people. Researchers from Meta Superintelligence Labs train MIMESIS, a 9B user simulator built from human conversations with explicit reasoning supervision and 13 behavioral patterns observed in real users, which beats Claude Opus 5 on behavioral fidelity by 13.4 points. Agents trained against the frozen simulator with multi-turn RL outperform agents trained against GPT-5.5 under all nine unseen user simulators, and a coaching step that turns the simulator's private reasoning into token-level feedback adds further gains. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/mimesis-learning-user-simulators-as-training-environments-for-interactive-agents-2610.09484), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2108588052914598084) |

## Top AI Papers of the Week (September 28 - October 4) - 2026
| **Paper**  | **Links** |
| ------------- | ------------- |
| 1) **Context Language Models** - Agent harnesses usually manage the model's context through fixed rules such as summarization or compaction. Researchers from Meta and collaborators propose Context Language Models (CLMs), which treat the live context as a file the model edits freely, deciding what to keep, rewrite, or remove. <br>● Context as a read-write file: The model edits its own context with code, writing loops that prune irrelevant results, defining functions it reuses, and tracking subagents. Recursive language models place a large input in an external variable the model can read, but they do not let it edit its live interaction context. Because several agent contexts can coexist as files, the approach extends to multi-agent systems.  <br>● Works zero-shot: Built from existing models with no training, CLMs reach 11.4% higher accuracy with 21.5% fewer FLOPs on BrowseComp-Plus than state-of-the-art context-management strategies, 5% higher scores with 59% fewer FLOPs on the 12-hour EdgeBench, and 65% more improvement for the same compute on a 24-hour agent-swarm task across six repositories.  <br>● Learnable in context and in weights: Natural-language instructions evolved through a skill-optimization loop raise held-out accuracy by up to 35.9 points on a context-management task. An online RL method improves Qwen3.5-9B on BrowseComp-Plus by 47.6% while using 12% fewer FLOPs.  <br>● Why it matters: Edits in the middle of the context break prefix caching, so the authors co-design Suffix Cache Reuse, which cuts server-side compute by 35% against standard SGLang at matched performance. Context management can move from the harness into the model without raising serving cost. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/context-language-models-2609.37725), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2105690460429996366) |
| 2) **JAZ** - Long-term memory and self-improvement are usually built as separate systems around the agent loop. Researchers from MIT CSAIL built JAZ to test how far a minimal harness, little more than the agent loop itself, can go on the tasks those systems target. <br>● One primitive: JAZ exposes a single LLM-based primitive, invoke, which acts as a function whose implementation the LLM writes at runtime each time it is called. Built-in hooks let the programmer apply constraints and monitor the run.  <br>● Everything is a variable: The LLM can write arbitrary executable code, including recursive calls to invoke, so subagents are the default. All inputs to invoke and the full interaction history live as variables in the code environment, where the model can read and transform them with code.  <br>● Beats specialized systems: With prompting only and no manually designed tools, memory system, or file system, JAZ outperforms Letta (MemGPT) by 8% at half its cost on the recall-heavy portion of StuLife, a long-horizon benchmark that requires recall far beyond the context window. On AppWorld it beats ACE, a continual self-improvement method, by 4% at lower cost.  <br>● Why it matters: Memory and self-improvement become code the agent writes inside its own loop. If you build custom harnesses, this is evidence that a smaller surface can match heavier infrastructure on these workloads. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/harness-as-a-language-a-minimalist-agent-framework-with-maximal-expressivity-2609.26891), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2103826930181308720) |
| 3) **Agensh** - Multi-agent harnesses usually depend on a central orchestrator that assigns tasks and coordinates workers, and that orchestrator limits how many agents the system can use. Microsoft Research introduces Agensh, a self-organized multi-agent harness with no central orchestrator, and scales it to 1,024 coding agents. <br>● A shared cooperation loop: Every worker runs the same loop asynchronously. It gathers context, claims and self-assigns a sub-task, takes action and shares findings, verifies the result, and merges progress.  <br>● Organization infrastructure: Three components support the loop. A shared workspace holds proposed, ongoing, and completed work, a message interface lets workers communicate, and a shared context retains reusable findings and work intentions.  <br>● Scaling the number of agents: On the five hardest ProgramBench tasks with GPT-5.6-sol, going from 1 to 128 agents raises the mean final test-pass rate from 19.31% to 28.78%, about a 49% relative improvement, and larger teams reach a given pass rate sooner. On building pandoc from scratch under a 6-hour budget, going from 1 to 1,024 agents raises the test-pass rate from 33.89% to 55.06%.  <br>● Why it matters: Worker trajectories show forms of cooperation that the agents start on their own and that become standard practice as the organization grows. The authors treat the number of agents as a new scaling dimension, which is most useful for complex tasks under hard latency limits or time budgets. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/agensh-scaling-organizational-intelligence-to-1-024-agents-2609.26781), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2104377054829613473) |
| 4) **AutoGym** - Training agents with RL requires a gym, meaning a task, an executable environment to attempt it in, and a verifier that reliably separates success from failure. These gyms are still built by hand, saturate as models improve, and get exposed to contamination. Researchers from Amazon AGI present AutoGym, which generates complete gyms from a minimal domain seed or from prior model trajectories. <br>● Blueprint first: AutoGym specifies the valid solution space, environment requirements, and verification criteria before it builds the environment. Solvability is settled during construction, so no unreliable LLM judge has to decide correctness afterward.  <br>● Difficulty you can steer: Explicit generation parameters control task topology, interaction depth, capability axes, question obfuscation, and distractor composition. Single-pass synthetic tasks tend to be hard only in their phrasing, and these parameters target actual difficulty.  <br>● Active curriculum: Performance-informed calibration shifts the distribution over those parameters as model capabilities change, and failure analysis feeds recurring capability gaps back into the generation parameters.  <br>● Why it matters: Across productivity and temporal-reasoning settings, AutoGym produces gyms spanning the capability spectrum, including instances that challenge frontier models. Building RL environments is becoming a core AI engineering skill, and this gives a recipe for gyms that keep pace with the models trained on them. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/autogym-blueprint-first-generation-of-verifiable-agent-gyms-2609.22592), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2104746239061586055) |
| 5) **CASD** - Search-based prompt optimizers such as GEPA propose edits, run fresh rollouts, score them, and keep only the edits that improve a validation metric. Researchers from Microsoft show that this loop may be unnecessary when you already have a corpus of agent trajectories. <br>● One pass over the logs: Coding-Agent Skill Distillation (CASD) gives an off-the-shelf coding agent a static corpus of trajectories. The agent writes and runs analysis code to compute corpus-wide statistics, finds systematic failure modes, inspects representative episodes, and distills the findings into behavioral rules in one prompt. It needs no environment access and no validation data.  <br>● Reflection scope: Search-based optimizers reflect on small batches of trajectories at each step, so failures that only show up across the whole corpus stay invisible to them. CASD reads the whole corpus with code, so it can find those failures.  <br>● Better and cheaper: Across ALFWorld, tau2-bench retail and telecom, and SpreadsheetBench-Verified, a single CASD pass improves the unoptimized baseline by 16.6 points on average, against 10.9 for GEPA and 5.3 for SkillOpt. Each optimized prompt costs about $1.60, over 22x cheaper than validation-gated search.  <br>● Why it matters: Even when GEPA and SkillOpt also get validation data and unrestricted environment access, CASD stays ahead on two of four benchmarks. If your agents already produce logs, pointing a coding agent at them is a cheap first step before running a search-based optimizer. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/coding-agents-are-strong-prompt-optimizers-2609.26261), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2103479392106352910) |
| 6) **Taste-Bench** - On long-horizon tasks, decisions such as which hypothesis to test or which implementation to build on determine how the whole run turns out. Researchers from Microsoft and collaborators call the ability to make these decisions well an agent's taste, and build Taste-Bench to measure it. <br>● Decision forks: Each question shows a point in a trajectory where several directions are open and one leads to a better outcome. The model picks a direction without seeing what happens after the fork.  <br>● Mined automatically: Forks come from engineering and research runs, either from parallel attempts at the same task or from detours inside a single trajectory. Filters drop forks that are guessable from the options alone and forks that cannot be decided from the context, so no human annotation is needed.  <br>● Just under 60%: The best model answers 59.7% of questions correctly. Forks whose deciding evidence appears later in the trajectory are much harder for every model, and a larger reasoning budget does not improve accuracy.  <br>● Why it matters: Taste can be trained. The authors distill the judgment of a teacher that has seen the outcome into a student model, which makes better decisions on unseen tasks and improves end-to-end success on held-out SWE-bench Pro tasks. Benchmarks that score only end-to-end success miss this capability, and for recursive self-improvement an agent's choice of direction also matters. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/the-tasteful-agent-measuring-and-improving-taste-in-long-horizon-tasks-2609.25804), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2103478384584278502) |
| 7) **Jev-Mem** - Many agentic memory systems use an autoregressive LLM to decide how memories are organized, retrieved, and used, which puts expensive generation on the critical path of every memory operation. Jev-Mem borrows its design from System-One/System-Two cognition and hands those decisions to a lightweight controller. <br>● Three planes: A System-One control plane makes the fast decisions, a structured multi-relational memory plane stores semantic, temporal, causal, and entity relations, and a System-Two reasoning plane handles slower, deliberative reasoning.  <br>● The controller runs memory: When storing, the controller assigns memory types and relations. When reading, it handles query routing, retrieval-budget allocation, graph traversal, candidate scoring, and adaptive stopping. The LLM is called only for complex reasoning and writing the final answer.  <br>● Faster and more accurate: On LoCoMo, Jev-Mem scores 0.777 overall with an LLM judge, an 11.0% relative improvement over the strongest baseline. Memory construction takes 158 seconds, 6.6x faster than the fastest competing memory system, and average query latency drops 36.7% to 0.93 seconds.  <br>● Why it matters: Memory latency adds up across every step of a long-horizon agent. Moving routine memory decisions to a small controller lowers that cost while improving answer quality, and the same split could apply to other harness decisions that a frontier model currently makes. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/jev-mem-system-one-controlled-agentic-memory-for-efficient-ai-agents-2609.23986), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2103603205821366311) |
| 8) **Critical-State RL** - Salesforce AI Research's Critical-State RL finds the one call in a multi-turn tool-use interaction where training helps, since reward variation that depends on later turns often reflects downstream randomness instead of the current action. It uses nested sampling to separate action-dependent reward variation from continuation noise, then trains only the selected call with contextual-bandit updates. On BFCL v4 missing-function tasks, training the selected turn adds about 14 points, while training the alternative turn leaves accuracy flat or worse. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/critical-state-rl-diagnosing-trainable-states-for-multi-turn-tool-use-2609.24985), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2104597506835530112) |
| 9) **SkillGym** - SkillGym turns human-written agent skills into 2,756 verifiable training environments across 12 categories, each with code-based checkers, and collects 8,364 successful trajectories for fine-tuning. Under Claude Code, fine-tuning Qwen3.5-35B-A3B adds 19.10 points on Terminal-Bench 2.1 and 28.13 points on skill-assisted SkillsBench v1.1, where it reaches 51.47%, above the reported scores for Claude Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-5.4 Mini. With no skills loaded, the trained model still beats the base model that has the skills in context. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/skillgym-internalizing-human-skills-into-llms-for-real-world-problem-solving-2609.27717), [Tweet](https://x.com/dair_ai/status/2104339055194546595) |
| 10) **AutoCompact** - AutoCompact trains a coding agent to decide when to compact its context, what working state to keep, and how to continue afterward, as part of its own policy. A judge reviews the base agent's compaction decisions and replaces flawed ones before they execute, and the corrected trajectories are used for SFT and then for RL that optimizes coding and compaction together on task success. Pass rates rise by 9.2 points on SWE-bench Verified and 5.0 points on SWE-PolyBench Verified, and the gains hold both with a 256K window that never overflows and with a 16K window that falls back to forced compaction. | [Paper](https://academy.dair.ai/papers/autocompact-learning-when-to-compact-context-in-long-horizon-coding-agents-2610.02163), [Tweet](https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2106416745686995025) |
